ParlaMint-NO_2019-06-04.ana.ud396e204	First of all, I would like to thank the Committee for a good, lengthy and thorough cooperation in this matter. It may be more rare for a matter to be dealt with over a period of 1.5 years in the Committee, but this probably reflects the scope and complexity of this matter. The Committee has tried in its work to meet the many questions and recordings that have emerged along the way, with the utmost seriousness and respect and emphasis on assessing these carefully before delivering the cases. July 22nd-saka raised a wide-ranging discussion of criminal law rules on being imputable and on the role of legal psychiatry in deciding whether the defendant is accountable. The calculation selection was named at kgl. Res. of 25 January 2013 and was mandated to assess criminal law rules in order to be imputable, psychiatric experts and the specific reactions. The selection delivered the announcement in NOU 2014 on 28 October 2014:10 Guilty, expertise and social protection, which was then sent to the audience. The most important changes in criminal law and criminal law deal with the fundamental regulation of accountability and indebtedness where the Department proposes the offence of criminal law, cf. Paragraph 20, second paragraph, point (a) ‘psychotic’, is replaced by the cover ‘serious mental illness’. Occurrence is a non-medical, legal basis and shall include both serious mental, somatic and organic suffering with mental effects. The background to the proposal is inter alia: that it is difficult to distinguish between medical and legal psychosis interventions, and that many people with psychosis diagnoses are not psychotic by criminal law. In addition, an additional condition is established that the person is not imputable because of the suffering and that the right in his assessment is to emphasise the degree of failure in the person’s reality and function. This would indicate that the weight of the symptom at the time of action is decisive, not whether the person has a diagnosis and that it is the right, not the experts, who decide the issue of debt. The threshold for the use of special responses in the healthcare system is delayed and, in order to strengthen social protection, a distinction is made between serious and minor offences proposed for deletion as a basis for special reaction, so that the future danger the offender represents is decisive, not what the offender has committed. The requirement for flexibility and competence is strengthened and new rules for the mandate of the experts and for the declarations of experts are introduced. It is also proposed to extend the right of experts to access relevant health information. There is a separate regulation on legal psychiatric examinations and experts and a new nationwide organisation of legal psychiatry on the model of the pilot project tested at Brøset. The Government Party is generally positive and satisfied with the changes proposed by the Department in criminal law and criminal law, and sees that the legislative changes will lead to a greater level of certainty for the assessment of criminal offences, strengthen social protection and improve the quality of legal psychiatric expert work. This is important both for the legal security of individuals and for society as a whole. However, we have recorded the criticism that criminal law’s original expression ‘psychotic’ in the proposition is proposed to be changed to ‘serious mental disorder’. The critics of the proposal have shown that the expression can create confusion about the difference between the understanding of the term ‘serious mental illness’ in criminal law and the similar term used in mental health protection law. We believe that by changing the expression ‘serious mental disorder’ to the more generalized expression ‘strongly abnormal mental state’, one avoids such confusion. We also stress that it is important to ensure that in practice the right does not leave the experts to decide whether a person is to be considered to be unreliable and therefore to be exempted from punishment. A clearer distinction between the general-language legal term and the medical professional term is necessary to ensure this. In addition, we would like the amendment to counteract unfortunate links and associations between mental illness and criminal behaviour. Finally, I would like to stress that the expression ‘severely abnormal state of mind’ shall be understood in the same way as the expression ‘serious mental disorder’ used in the proposition and thus does not imply any change in reality. I would like to address the proposal from the Right and the Advance Party.

In addition, a key aspect of this legislative work is the need to strengthen social protection and improve the quality of legal psychiatric expertise. A minimum content requirement is introduced for the mandate of the experts in the case of a judicial psychiatric examination, as well as minimum rules on the design of the statement of experts. The introduction of these requirements will clearly enhance the quality of the work of the experts and contribute more to more accurate and verifiable judgments in the courts. ParlaMint-NO_2019-06-04.ana.ud396e304	The July 22 case revealed weaknesses in the criminal law rules on inadmissibility and created a debate on the influence of legal psychiatrists on whether the defendants are inconceivable. On that basis, the Assets Committee was established and they made their investigation on 28 October 2014. The proposition to be taken into account by the Storting is a follow-up to the proposals in that investigation. I note that the majority of the Judicial Committee does not support the government's proposal and that the majority proposes to send the proposition back to the government. At the same time, I am pleased that a minority in the Committee on Justice, the representatives of the Right and the Progressive Party support the proposal. I think it is important for the follow-up after 22 July that these proposals are now adopted. There's several reasons for that. Firstly, it is important to put in place a consistent rule on who should be exempted from punishment because of impunity. Secondly, it is important to strengthen social protection. Thirdly, it is important to improve the quality of legal psychiatric expertise. With regard to the rules of criminal law on inviolability resulting from mental illness, the government proposes that the term «psychotic» be replaced by the term «serious mental illness». This shall include mental, somatic and organic disorders with effects on the mind. The legislative proposal lays down additional conditions under which persons must be unpredictable because of this suffering, and it is further expressly stated in the law that, in the case of discretionary judgement, the right must emphasise the degree of failure of the person’s understanding of reality and functioning. I have noticed that the minority of the Judicial Committee proposes to replace the condition ‘serious mental disorder’ by ‘strongly abnormal mental state’. As a result of the additional conditions I have just mentioned, such a change will not have a bearing on the severity of the overall amount of symptoms required in order to be free from punishment. I therefore have no strong contradictions with the change that the minority has made. There are several problems with today's rule. Firstly, it affects both too many and too few people — too many because psychosis diagnosis can lead to the imposition of impunity even though the person’s understanding of reality at the time of action is not so dire that he cannot be blamed for the crime. It affects too few people who have a mental state of deformity that is equally disruptive to the perpetrator's sense of reality and motivation that psychosis is, can be punished today. Examples include some serious cases of autism and dementia. On the basis of the rules on the non-punishment of the indebted person, this appears unreasonable. The government’s proposal will also make it clear that the weight of the symptoms at the time of action is essential to exempt him from punishment, and not whether the person has a particular diagnosis. Another problem of current practice is that it is the experts who define the content of one of the basic terms of the penal code. Such practice is not suitable for ensuring legal clearance and legal entity. On the other hand, the Government's proposal would make it clear that the right to determine the content of the rule of inadmissibility, rather than the experts, is the right. Transferring the power of definition to justice will facilitate the development, through case-law, of a fixed and uniform threshold as to how sick or how deviant one must be to be considered to be unpredictable. In order to ensure such a development, I believe, like the minority of the Judicial Committee, that it is absolutely essential that the terms of the law be described in general terms and not through a medical term, as psychotic. In the light of the criticism received by the government in the public debate, I would like to stress: the proposal will not imply a relativisation of the threshold in order to be regarded as improbable, so that the person goes free, while another person who is equally ill will be punished. As the Legal Affairs Committee minority points out in the vote, the threshold is general and applies to all. It is crucial that the perpetrator is so ill that he reaches the threshold.

ParlaMint-NO_2019-06-04.ana.ud396e498	The Committee has considered Prop. 100 L for 2018–2019, Changes in criminal law, etc., terror-related international obligations, etc., where the Ministry proposes legislative changes that follow up international obligations and recommendations in the area of terrorism. I would like to thank the Committee for its speedy examination of the proposal and the concrete amendments proposed by the government, which an overall committee seems to be behind. Today’s proposals are about travel for terrorist purposes, acts of terrorism against aviation, the binding of economic resources and the rules of the Collection Act on professional qualifications. It facilitates the ratification by Norway of the Riga Protocol through a specialised criminal order against travel for terrorist purposes and a legislative amendment which clarifies that the financing of travel for the purpose of providing or receiving terrorist training can be penalised as terrorist financing. These are amendments implementing international obligations under UN Security Council Resolution 2178 and the Riga Protocol to the Council of Europe Convention on the Prevention of Terrorism. Furthermore, amendments to the penal code aimed at terrorist acts against aviation have been proposed. The Proposition proposes to criminalise the hazardous use of aircraft, as well as the use and release of certain weapons and hazardous materials from aircraft. In addition, minor adjustments are made to the criminal provisions on hijacking. The proposed amendments will facilitate the accession of Norway to the Beijing Convention to combat acts of unlawful interference related to civil aviation security and the Beijing Protocol to combat the illegal use of aircraft, further developing conventions already in force in Norway. Changes have also been proposed to the rules on the bonding of economic resources, which facilitate a more effective and safe use of that measure. These proposals are based mainly on an evaluation report on Norway, issued in 2014 by the Financial Action Task Force, FATF. The transfer of this legislation from the Criminal Procedures Act to the Police Act has been proposed in order to comply with international recommendations that binding should be independent of criminal proceedings. Banding shall no longer be a criminal offence, but shall be decided by the Police Security Service as a non-enforcement measure based on the Police Act. In addition to these proposals, changes are also made to the collection law, and the correction of incubators in the law.
ParlaMint-NO_2019-06-04.ana.ud396e513	I am also very pleased that today we are dealing with and adopting these changes, and that a total increase is behind this. As the rapporteur pointed out, this proposition involves several changes, including several important changes, in terms of punishing and preventing terrorism. Some of these changes stem from the UN Security Council Resolution 2178 of five years ago, which the then President Obama himself led, is about obligations to prevent and prevent foreign fighters and about criminalising travel with terror as its object. The time we are now in and the development we have seen of IS lately shows that it is important and necessary that the international community stands together and has equal rules in this area, so that we do not see again the same kind of blossoming of foreign warriors as we have seen with the Islamic State. I am therefore very pleased that the Stortinge today adopts more intrusions and new punishments in this area. It is also very good that we update the law and take over the fact that the technology is changing, the time is changing. Where violence was used in the past as an opportunity to take over aircraft and ships, today it can be done through technology – without making it any minor form of terror or assault on civilians and others affected. I am therefore very pleased that the Storting decided this today. This is something we need in the time we live in now.
ParlaMint-NO_2019-06-04.ana.ud396e524	Today, the government is proposing changes to the law that ensure that we comply with international obligations and recommendations in the area of terrorism, and it is gratifying that there is an overall judicial committee behind the proposal. It is important that we support international cooperation against terrorism and follow up on the criminalisation obligations contained in the international agreements. The aim of the proposed changes is to make visible to a large extent the loyal implementation of our international commitments and also the fact that some of the proposals involve a new criminalisation. Firstly, the government is proposing a special penal order against travel for terrorist purposes. It has an important signal effect that we get our own penalty order of this kind of act. The use of hidden coercive measures could also be essential for the effective enforcement of the ban on terrorist travel and for the prevention of such travel. Therefore, in the event of an infringement of the criminal order for terrorist travel, it shall be possible to use unannounced search, technical sounding equipment in clothing, items, etc., communication interception and data reading. On the other hand, it is not proposed to open up space listening. The government also proposes a legislative amendment which makes it clear that funding travel for the purpose of providing or receiving training in terrorism can be penalised as terrorist financing. These changes ensure that we fulfil the obligations under UNSCR 2178 and the Riga Protocol of the Council of Europe Convention on Terrorism. Secondly, the government is proposing changes to the penal code aimed at acts of terrorism against aviation. The Criminal Code will have a specialised provision on the hazardous use of aircraft and the use and release of certain types of weapons and hazardous materials from aircraft. The government also proposes a certain extension of the hijacking rules, including: the provision concerns hijacking carried out for a longer period than before, from the start of the preparation of the aeroplane to approximately one day after it has landed. It is also made clear that hijacking involves obtaining control of an aeroplane using technological aids. Furthermore, there will therefore no longer be a requirement for the perpetrator to be on board the aeroplane in order for the action to be covered by the provision. The legislative changes facilitate Norway’s accession to the Beijing Convention of 2010 on combating illegal acts related to civil aviation security and to the Beijing Protocol of 2010 on combating the illegal use of aircraft. Thirdly, amendments to the rules on binding are proposed. Bonding means that all assets belonging to a person are frozen, e.g.: by blocking bank accounts. Banding may be carried out if someone for good reason is presumed to have committed an act which is subject to specified terrorist provisions in the Criminal Code. The rules on the bonding of economic resources are proposed to be moved from the Criminal Procedures Act to the Police Act. The aim of the amendment is to comply with international recommendations that binding be independent of criminal proceedings. Other amendments to the rules on binding are also proposed, which facilitate a more effective and legally sound use of the measure. The Government is pleased to present this proposal which ensures that Norway fulfils its international obligations and supports it during international cooperation against terrorism.

ParlaMint-NO_2019-06-04.ana.ud396e605	The possibility for the court to review a conviction in appeal is a fundamental requirement for legal certainty and the human rights principle that Norway is obliged to follow. Today, the appeal to the Lawyers' Court may be scrutinised by an appeal committee which rejects or refers the appeal to full treatment, on the condition that the appeal committee has to carry out a real review of the judgment of the Court of First Instance in the light of what is stated in the appeal. Reasons must be given for the decision. Since the two-institutional reform in 1995, the convictions for offences with a penalty framework of more than six years have had an unconditional right to ordinary appeals. The Government is now proposing to abolish this right as a follow-up to the majority of the Criminal Procedures Committee. The government proposes that cases of more than six years of imprisonment in criminal law should also be subject to so-called appeals. Like the Workers’ Party, the Centre has the country not to support the government’s proposal, but will instead make an alternative proposal to limit unconditionally the right to appeal to cases with a penalty period of more than six years, where it has been claimed or sentenced to imprisonment for more than three years, detention or transfer to forced mental health protection or forced care. The criminal law commission was divided into the view of appeals. The minority pointed out that the main argument for introducing appeals for all cases has been saved on resource use, but also noted that the legal security aspect has not been adequately addressed in the majority’s reasoning. The importance of legal certainty is opposed to the effectiveness and use of resources in this case. The central party understands the government's desire for an effective judicial process without undue use of resources, for an unconditional right to appeals always gives rise to a risk that cases that are clearly not going to lead to full proceedings. However, we believe that the proposal, which is being given a majority today, goes too far in challenging legal certainty. The full appeal procedure for some cases that are clearly not likely to be brought is a small price to be paid to ensure the conviction of legal certainty in the most serious cases. Punishment is the most invasive coercion society can impose on a citizen. We have had ugly examples of murders in Norway in recent times, and we should do what we can to prevent it from happening again. Then the appeal system is very important, especially in the most serious cases. The minority of the criminal justice commission has pointed out that the amount of resources saved by extending the appeal procedure is unclear. The parties often use a lot of resources to promote an appeal to the law, which can be expected to increase if the unconditional access to appeal is removed for the most serious cases. Another question is whether law enforcement rights will have the possibility and the will to limit the rejection of anchors to what is actually intended, namely anchors that will clearly not produce. Firstly, the rights of the team members do not have a complete basis for decision when dealing with them in the appeal committee. The file received by the appeal committee is not intended to be a full basis for deciding and answering the guilt question in a case. Secondly, we may risk taking labour pressure and capacity in the courts as a harmless consideration in the appeal proceedings. Lawyers' rights have a very demanding workload and sometimes a very long procedure. For example, in 2018, the length of proceedings for evidence of more than six years was eight months in Borgarting Lawyers' Law and seven months in Eidsivating Lawyers' Law. A total of 383 cases were dealt with in the law courts in 2018, but the number of pending cases increased and was on 201 cases. These were evidence of six years of detention in 2018. In a demanding resource situation with high labour pressure, it may be tempting to facilitate the work situation on slipping out and rejecting anchors. The Courts have been in a difficult economic situation for a long time and have received large budget cuts due, inter alia, to: the red tape and efficiency reform. The centre party believes that removing the general appeal procedure for the most serious cases would be to go too far to achieve a chronic savings. It could threaten legal certainty. Instead of removing such a very important legal certainty guarantee in order to save money, the government should increase the allocation to the courts.
ParlaMint-NO_2019-06-04.ana.ud396e626	The main position of the SV in this case is that suspects in the most serious criminal cases should be able to try their case with full main proceedings in two instances. I am therefore pleased that the minority of the Committee, represented at the Right and the Advance Party, are behind the government's proposal. If the bill is adopted today, there will be a step in the right direction towards more effective and secure criminal proceedings where the resources of the courts are used where they are most needed.

ParlaMint-NO_2019-06-04.ana.ud396e782	I would like to thank the Committee for a thorough work on the recommendation for Prop. 68 L for 2018-2019. The change involves a new residence permit in the Foreigners’ Act which has been refused protection and which has not been granted a humanitarian residence permit. As a result of these changes, the government also proposes an amendment to the law to maintain access to temporary work permits. Incurries are also proposed in Sections 32 and 42 of the Foreign Affairs Act. In order to address the issue of expulsion in cases where an application for protection is rejected by the Directorate of Foreign Affairs and the Department of Foreign Affairs, a number of assessments have to be made today before such an additional decision on expulsion can be taken. Therefore, there is reason to amend the rules in order to avoid spending time in all asylum cases on assessments which are not relevant to the issue of protection and which are generally of limited importance, in view of the few cases in which expulsion under the rules is actually required. The proposal is intended to simplify and streamline the procedure in asylum matters. This is important not only for economics and administration, but also for those people who are waiting for a clarification of their application. I would like to point out that the proposal was supported by Amnesty International, the Association of Lawyers, the NOAS, the Directorate of Foreign Affairs, the Foreign Affairs Department and the Directorate of Police when the Ministry had the proposal at the hearing. Current rules, which make it inappropriate for time to be spent in all asylum cases on assessments not relevant to the issue of protection. The proposed amendment would result in fewer assessments of issues that are not relevant to the asylum procedure itself. This will simplify and thus streamline the procedure. Most of the additional decisions on expulsion were taken today in matters relating to protection under Section 32 of the Foreign Affairs Act, i.e. Dublin cases. These are complicated by today’s rules on expulsion as an additional decision. Furthermore, it is important to stress that simplified legislation can make the procedure more effective, compared with today. This means that less time per case will be spent, time that can be used to deal with several similar cases during the same period. This will lead to better use of resources in the management of non-residents.

ParlaMint-NO_2019-06-04.ana.ud396e814	The representative, Karin Andersen, has drawn up the proposal to which she referred.
ParlaMint-NO_2019-06-04.ana.ud396e819	Bill 68 L, presented to the Stortinget today, contains proposals for amendments to the Foreign Affairs Act. The changes are, to a large extent, a simplification of legislation, with the result that the overall time-use of asylum decision-making can be somewhat reduced. In my opinion, this will lead to a better use of resources in the foreign administration. When the foreign authorities today reject an application for protection, an additional decision on expulsion is usually taken or an additional registration is made that the foreigner has to leave Norway. A refusal decision is an administrative response which requires the foreign citizen to leave the country or to be refused entry. However, the expulsion decision does not prohibit future entry and the person may return to Norway at any time provided that the grounds for refusal no longer exist. No rejection decision is necessary in order to implement a refusal of protection. However, expulsion decisions are necessary for the immediate implementation of a negative decision if the foreigner has another valid temporary residence basis in Norway, such as: a visa. It is also necessary when the applicant has already received protection in another country participating in Dublin cooperation. The non-resident authorities have indicated the need to simplify and streamline the rules on expulsion in the context of refusal of protection applications. Under current legislation, a number of assessments have to be made if such an additional expulsion decision is to be taken, such as whether or not he/she had valid travel documents on arrival in Norway, assumed residence before the adoption of the decisions, and the purpose of the stay here. The assessment to be made in relation to the expulsion decision in these cases has nothing to do with protection issues. Therefore, I do not consider it appropriate to spend a lot of time on these additional assessments in all these asylum cases and propose an authorisation for rejection of the application for protection under Section 17 of the Foreign Affairs Act. And, as we have also heard, this has got quite broad approval from the hearing bodies. As a consequence of the proposed simplification, there is a need to amend the provisions of the Foreign Affairs Act that regulate the possibility of temporary work and work permit pending the examination of the application for protection, in order to maintain this possibility. The amendment implies that, provided that other conditions are also met, temporary work permits may be granted if the expulsion decision is unlikely to be effective before the applicant has been in Norway for six months. I have chosen to set the residence time to six months, so that the time limit in the provision should have a practical reality.

ParlaMint-NO_2019-06-04.ana.ud396e1515	At the request of the Transport and Communications Committee, the President will propose that speaking time be limited to 5 minutes to each party group and 5 minutes to members of the Government. In addition, the President will propose that, within the allocated speaking time, up to six rows be given, with replies from members of the Government, and that those on the list of speakers, in addition to the allocated speaking time, have a speaking time of up to 3 minutes. – It shall be deemed to have been adopted.
ParlaMint-NO_2019-06-04.ana.ud396e1525	There has long been a desire to improve the taxi offer. It's not that everything is very well with today's system, so you can get the impression in the debate. Several developments have led the Ministry of Transport to take a closer look at the taxi market and the taxi regulation. There are high entry barriers, low competition, the offer is not adapted to demand, and taxi operators have long waiting times and a lot of empty driving. Together with low price information, this contributes to high prices for customers. The framework conditions for taxi transport have changed significantly since the regulation of taxis was introduced. There is a great technological development in the market, and our neighbouring countries are liberalizing their taxi markets. The main challenge of making changes to the Professional Transport Act is the big difference between central and districts. It is important that in a system of new taxi regulation there are opportunities to ensure a satisfactory taxi offer throughout the country. The law changes give the states a special responsibility for this part of the taxi policy. It is therefore important to ensure that county municipalities have tools to ensure good taxi coverage. It is clear from the law that this can be regulated by county regulations and the conclusion of exclusive agreements. The amendment of the law means that the ceiling on permissions lapses. It is important, however, to underline that there is still to be a requirement for authorisation for the carriage of passengers by vehicle of up to nine seats. The requirements for the holder of the authorisation are reduced slightly, but the requirement for a driving licence is tightened. There is also an intensification of the bill by the fact that one in Section 4 equates transport to the public sector, whether it is physical or via digital platforms. This closes an important loophole in the current law, which only attached the requirement of public space permits. In addition, the permits will have to be renewed every 10 years. This will allow more new players to enter the market, but will also help to remove the unprofessional ones. The person holding taxi licenses must himself meet all the objective requirements. In other words, pursue an actual and lasting activity in Norway, be of good repute and have a satisfactory financial capacity. The Ministry emphasises that the purpose of the requirement of satisfactory financial standing is to ensure that the creditor has a sufficiently orderly economy for the proper functioning of the business, including the ability to pay public taxes and charges. The requirement for taximeters in the taxis is maintained, but technical solutions other than today’s taximeters are open. The majority will point out that it is important that alternatives to taximeters also meet the objectives of taximeters as far as is appropriate and note that the Ministry proposes, until further notice, to continue the requirement for taximeters in the professional transport regulation of Section 48. It is a relatively extensive amendment to the Professional Transport Act and the Regulation, which will affect the individual taxi driver and industry. The waiver of the obligation to be connected to a central authority also means the removal of the right of association. However, the need for the centres or other forms of organisation does not go away. Until then, the ordering market is too important for the industry. However, what one can expect is that the majority of the actors will organise themselves in larger companies or use a larger distribution of taxi services. This could lead to professionalisation of the industry. Today, each owner of the privilege is self-employed, with the obligations that this entails. In future legislation, in our opinion, we are likely to get larger players with their own rights and employees drivers. In this way, more people will be able to get a professional employer, with the rights it implies to be employed. Today’s driver, who is employed by a privilege holder, is also given the opportunity to take out permission – if desired – or apply for a job with another business concept/centre. The main objective of the amendments to the Professional Transport Act is to create an industry that improves quality and price. It will be achieved by making it easier to recruit for the profession, but also to phase out the unprofessional. When customers get into a taxi, they should feel safe and know that they are going where they are going, at a pre-arranged price and in a timely manner.
ParlaMint-NO_2019-06-04.ana.ud396e1544	Many have their daily work of transporting people and goods on Norwegian roads. The Labour Party regards drivers as a professional group with the right of course to live on their job and to establish themselves with family, house and home. In this bill, the government is adding to a policy of temporary jobs and poor working conditions, and many fear that this part of the transport industry too will now develop into a market of undeclared work and unprofessionalism. The pressure on employment and wages already points to a level where the driver profession becomes impossible to live on. This development will not have the Workers' Party. The government has submitted a bill to remove the number limit on taxis and open the doors wide open for apps like Uber and Cabify. This will destroy the labour market of a large profession in the taxi industry. Both perpetrators and drivers are going to face major challenges if this bill is adopted, which unfortunately seems to be happening. People and families who need predictable income and the ability to secure their homes, apartments, future pensions and social security schemes must now be forced to compete against leisure drivers with a Uber app that is not aware that arranged conditions are fundamental rights in a modern society. The government takes the old-fashioned and dangerous labour society back to Norwegian passenger transport. The Labour Party is opposed to the proposal because there are greater differences, but there are also many other reasons to oppose the proposal, and there are many in this case. The expertise is quite clear in the hearing opinions. Norwegian Ecoforum has members from the tax administration, police, Nav, customs and others who work against economic crime, among others. in banking and insurance, and they warn against the proposal, which, in their view, will lead to wrong developments. They fear that the consequence will be that the industry will be brought back to the way it was before the great cleanup of the 1990s, with much undeclared work, tax evasion and financial crime. Nearly unmarked taxis shall now be open to leisure, without the competence of VAT, tax and working time. The Transport Economics Institute also warns against development, and the Oslo Municipality fears chaos in the center when cars without belonging to the central are standing along the pavement to plow customers. It is even the case that the Consumer Council, which supports liberalisation, warns: ‘The research and experience from countries where the needs test has been suspended shows that a release in the number of permits presents risks of over-establishment, increased prices in the individual customer market, lower quality of services and the centralisation of the service geographically and with regard to the concentration of taxi services at certain times of the day.’» That's very well said. The proposal is perceived by us as a kneecap for market-liberalist ideology in an area where it will not work. In addition, there's a crash for Uber, who's ruined large parts of the taxi industry all over the world, and they haven't earned a single penny yet. But it might have been an idea to talk to the parties, talk to the customers, talk to the experts, so that one could get a proposal that could carry out. That is why we also have a proposal that goes along with it, and I am taking up the proposals of the Workers' Party, the Centre Party and the SV.
ParlaMint-NO_2019-06-04.ana.ud396e1564	The representative Øystein Langholm Hansen then brought up the proposals he referred to.
ParlaMint-NO_2019-06-04.ana.ud396e1569	This debate on amendments to the Professional Transport Act which we are about to deal with has been going on for a long time. In short, there has been a matter that has created and continues to create great commitment. The contradictions between those who want to increase competition and liberalisation and those who do not want any change at all are great. But society is still changing. Products and businesses come and go. New technologies offer new opportunities, better services and better service both to the suppliers of goods and services and not least to the users or customers. A market where there has been a lot of regulation and little modernisation is the taxi market. It has, among other things, led to a lack of optimal functioning of the taxi market. It's not like the Workers' Party here claims, that the problems will arise with the changes we're making, and that the earnings will decrease. Regrettably, earnings have fallen over several years and have not been positive for the industry. You have experienced a loss of earnings, and it has not been positive for the customers either. In addition, the EFTA Surveillance Authority has pointed out that parts of the legislation are in direct conflict with the EEA Agreement. All of this demands on us politicians both in terms of their willingness and ability to make the necessary changes in order to adapt laws and regulations to today's society. The government has taken the consequences of this, and that is why we now have this case for consideration today. Competition is healthy, competition is right and competition is important. This is not how the Workers' Party tries to claim that competition is not working. They have also said that in the past when it comes to e.g.: public tasks such as road construction, but now I even think the Workers' Party sees New Roads working perfectly in competition with the State Road Service, and that tax money and tax money are getting better. It is completely wrong that competition only works for clothing stores, food shops, pubs and restaurants. Competition works largely in all areas of society, including the taxi industry. What is crucial is that competition takes place on a level playing field, and that's exactly why we set foot down for Uber in the first place when they tried, because we saw that it was unfair to today's established taxi market. The NTP states that the taxis are part of the public transport service and that the framework conditions must meet the needs of users for accessibility and safety. And this, too, the government is going away from now. The second mystery is that the proposition still refers to the EEA Agreement, to EEA law and to ESA. The ESA considers that the examination of the needs and the obligation of the holder of the privilege to be associated with a taxi centre is not in line with the right of free establishment under Article 31 of the EEA Agreement. However, it is clear that restrictions on the right of free establishment under the EEA Agreement are legal if justified for reasons of general interest — and there is a general interest in ensuring an adequate provision of passenger transport for the public. The changes that are now taking place are not because the EEA Agreement requires it, but because the government wants it. Why it wants to dismantle the taxi industry as part of public transport and ignore the needs of users for accessibility is a mystery. The third mystery is how representatives from the Right can go out in the newspapers and say that the main elements of today's legislation are being passed on, and trivialise the changes as some adaptations. Dismantling the need test/number regulation, discharging the obligation to operate, completing the central connection obligation, removing the requirement of financial permission guarantee, removing the current requirements of the privilege holder’s competence, removing the provision that the privilege holder must have a taxi as their principal occupation and removing taximeters and ceiling lamp are not minor changes. There are complete changes.

ParlaMint-NO_2019-06-04.ana.ud396e1805	Over the last few weeks, many of the changes that come for the taxi industry have been discussed. In such areas, the county may grant one or more companies the exclusive right to drive taxis in the municipalities concerned. For today's municipal structure this applies to 384 of the 422 municipalities in the country. Renewal is important for the future existence of the industry. We can't keep up the way we've always done, just because it's comfortable. The right would therefore like to welcome new thinking actors, as long as they comply with Norwegian laws and regulations. It is important to point out that all operators of the taxi industry have to register their activities in Norway. Developments in society also open up new opportunities for the taxi industry. We see a rapid development of public transport, where ordering transport is becoming more and more common. Several collective actors are trying out schemes like ‘Get Me’. That means that almost empty regular buses are replaced by smaller and more vehicles that pick people up where they are, bringing them to where they are going, when they want it. Here today’s taxi drivers will have a central role to play.

ParlaMint-NO_2019-06-04.ana.ud396e2106	At the request of the Transport and Communications Committee, the President will propose that speaking time be limited to 5 minutes to each party group and 5 minutes to members of the Government. ParlaMint-NO_2019-06-04.ana.ud396e2115	First of all, I would like to thank the Committee on Legal Affairs and Citizens' Rights for their good cooperation. Although it is not a unanimous committee that proposes a decision on the matter, it is likely that the Committee will agree on most of the direction presented here. The matter has received a lot of attention from the interested parties. Two consultation rounds have been carried out and a total of 135 consultation replies have been received. The proposed legislation is based on NOU 2018: 4 Sjöveien go on. The government has also submitted a plug-in to Prop. 86 L – Prop 108 L. In addition, the government explains that it has been revealed that in individual provisions of the Prop bill. 86 L is not prevented from continuing with some points in the current right. This concerns the Board of Appeal for individual decisions taken by the Municipality, the authorisation authority for measures which may affect defence and preparedness interests, and the basis for an infringement fee. In addition, the government proposes changes as a result of new border legislation. The legislative proposal involves the restructuring and simplification of the existing law, as well as a number of substantive changes. In the proposal for a new law, shared responsibility and authority for the waters between municipalities and states are continued. A specific authorisation is proposed to empower municipalities to regulate, inter alia: anchoring in its own sea area, but the regulations shall be approved by the Ministry. The responsibility and authority of navigation facilities and the responsibility for the remediation of the waters are proposed to the State as a whole. This will in principle result in a cost shift from municipality to state. It also provides for the Ministry to be empowered to regulate navigation in the waters and the municipalities to regulate the movement of recreational craft in their own sea area. Municipal voyage regulations for recreational craft will no longer be subject to approval by the Coastal Works. The coastal community currently spends a lot of resources on these approvals, and a change will lead to savings in the area. Today's law deals with three types of charges: coast charges, security charges and call charges. The proposal for a new law removes the possibility of levying coastal charges, given that this has been a dwindling provision since the closure of the coastal charge in 2013 as part of efforts to strengthen the competitiveness of maritime transport. Furthermore, it is proposed that a minor change be made to the provision for a safety charge by making the owner of the ship also responsible for the payment of the fee. It is therefore proposed that, in view of the fact that the municipalities will continue to carry out their tasks in the waters, the call fee should be continued under the name ‘water tax’. The basis for levying a water tax is reduced as a result of the proposal that the State assumes full responsibility for improvements in the waters. The provision is designed to clarify the tasks and measures that the municipality can finance through the levy and it will continue to be such that this levy is calculated on the basis of the principle of self-sufficiency. The proposal for a law allows for values to be shared out from port operations if adequate funds are allocated to proper port operations. The proposal provides for sound capital for municipally owned ports so that the port can maintain its position as an important infrastructure for maritime transport. In practice, it is assumed that for most ports, the requirement of sound equity would imply little or no change in relation to the current provision, since there is no room for dividends or other distribution from the port activity either today. However, for some of the largest ports with a high level of port capital, dividends may be available. Dividend access may also provide incentives for efficient port operations and active and sound ownership management. Municipalities will also be given incentives to sell properties that have a high value for alternative uses. Disposal of land could bring a net gain that could both raise capital for alternative port areas, generate surpluses that can be extracted and used for other tasks the municipality might have, and contribute to environmentally friendly urban and land development. It is proposed to introduce an infringement fee in response to minor infringements of the provisions laid down in or in compliance with the Port and Water Law. This could increase the effectiveness of regulatory enforcement and thus contribute to a reduction in the cost to the State. The threshold for the granting of an infringement fee is lower compared to the current situation. This may result in more than one response to the current law. The proposal for a new law does not continue to require adequate standards for port installations and discharges used in the operation of the port. This is because these types of requirements and regulations fall within the scope of the Planning and Building Act and the Working Environment Act. As a consequence, municipalities will no longer be subject to the port and water law. As mentioned at the outset, the Committee has worked well on the matter and initially agrees on most of the proposed legislation. However, an overall committee was not entirely satisfied with the government’s proposed legislative text on port capital, since it was not as clear as the Port Law Committee’s draft legislative text. An overall committee considers it important to ensure appropriate areas and a robust and long-term economy if ports are to contribute to achieving the transport policy objectives.

ParlaMint-NO_2019-06-04.ana.ud396e2956	To let go of a little sensation here before I start: in this case, there is an overall attitude from the committee. So then you're going to read what I'm saying, based on that. In the proposal, the Ministry of Transport proposes an amendment to the Law of 4 July 2003 No. 83 on electronic communications, the eco-nomy law, in order to regulate retail and wholesale charges for calls to special numbers, 5-digit numbers, so that the price level is lower, and that the numbering resource can be used for its purpose. It is also proposed to include a new provision in the Ecoom Act in order to set maximum prices for international number-based communications systems within the EEA territory. National communication authorities have for several years tried to contribute to greater competition on calls and tried to publish prices in dialogue with industry and through direct calls to operators. This has not led to lower prices for calls to special numbers. The Committee would stress that the high price level is a challenge not only for calls but also for calls to special numbers. As the receiver of calls, you want to be able to offer numbers that are easy to remember for calls, without customers having to pay overprice for the call. Another advantage of the 5-digit series is that there is a possibility of a corresponding SMS number. The Committee has noted that the high ring price reduces the demand for the special numbers. The Committee would stress that the proposed price regulation harmonises retail and wholesale prices for calls to special numbers and vice versa with end-users for calls to mobile numbers. The Committee points out that the Ministry expects that the proposed regulation could gradually stimulate the increased use of special numbers, which are desirable. The Committee would point out that over several years, there have been challenges with Norwegian special numbers being difficult to obtain from EEA countries. This is due, inter alia, to: high termination prices. The regulation of retail and termination charges for special call numbers is a necessary measure. The Committee is also aware of the fact that a decision has been taken in the EU on the ecosystem, and this will also lead to a desire to have an equal opportunity both in the EU and in Norway at the same prices. Similar national rules are needed to provide Norwegian consumers with equivalent protection against unfairly high prices from the same time as EU citizens. The Committee points out that experience in dealing with extensive market decisions in the broadband and mobile markets has made it apparent that there is a need for a derogation from the three-month period laid down in the Administrative Act. The Committee also draws attention to the experience gained from the Ministry’s appeal proceedings, which suggests that smaller market players often do not have sufficient resources to care for their own interests. So this is an overall setting.

ParlaMint-NO_2019-06-04.ana.ud396e2984	Today, we are discussing the proposal of the Ministry of Transport on changes in the law on electronic communications in order to be able to regulate the producer and wholesale prices for calling the special number – five-digit number etc. – in regulations, so that the price level is lower, and that the number resource can be used for its purpose – customer service. It is also proposed to include a new eco-label to set maximum prices for international number-based communication services within the EEA territory in the regulation. The change is intended to ensure that Norwegian consumers benefit from reduced prices of international calls, from both fixed and mobile networks, and SMS from the same time as EU citizens or as close to this time as possible. National communications authorities have for several years tried to contribute to greater competition in calling for special numbers by publishing prices, dialogue with industry and direct calls to providers. However, there have been no lower calls to special numbers, and there is no indication that the providers will award calls to special numbers equal to the basic tariffs on their own initiative. The Ministry is also aware that over several years it has our challenges that Norwegian special numbers are difficult to obtain from EEA countries. It is due, inter alia, to: high termination rates. Regulationing the end-user and termination prices for calling the special number may therefore be a central factor and a necessary and proportionate measure to increase the availability of Norwegian special numbers within the EEA territory. It is therefore surprising to note from the opposition that they would like the government to consider whether it should be possible to derogate from the maximum prices in order to ensure Norwegian ownership and Norwegian employment. This means that Norwegian consumers have to pay more than the maximum price and do not have the same protection against unreasonably high prices as other EU/EEA citizens. In the opinion of the Right, it is not the right way to appraise the rights of citizens, and we recommend adopting changes in the eco-lovables as they are produced by a single committee.

ParlaMint-NO_2019-06-04.ana.ud396e3325	The representative Nina Sandberg has taken up the suggestions she referred to.
ParlaMint-NO_2019-06-04.ana.ud396e3330	With a positive decision in relation to the treatment of Prop. 89 L for 2018–2019, we are today legislating students' access to student representatives at all universities, colleges and colleges – this primarily as a result of the students' persistent efforts over the years. The aim of the law is for all students to have access to a student representative. The Student Ombudsman system is currently not regulated, either by the Law on Higher Education or by the Law on Vocational Education. Nevertheless, today, ten higher education institutions have set up student offices on their own initiative and all these institutions are state-owned. The scheme has proved to be beneficial for the students and for the institutions. For the post-secondary education sector, the student rights have been significantly strengthened in the last few years by the Fachskolegesetz. These changes have resulted in the rights of post-secondary students being essentially equal to those of university and higher education students, as well as in this case. The mission of the Student Ombudsmen is inter alia: to help guide students in complaints, to help resolve matters related to their study situation, at the lowest possible level, and to bring the matter before the Board of Directors or into the institution's quality assurance work. The professional independence of the ombudsmen is a fundamental condition of the ombudsman regime. This means that the student representative cannot be instructed in his/her function. However, we believe that institutions should have a great deal of freedom to choose solutions tailored to local circumstances and needs, and institutions should be free to choose the organisation of the student representative scheme themselves. This includes, inter alia: smaller institutions can cooperate with other institutions in order to establish and offer a joint arrangement with student representatives. Individual institutions may also lay down competence requirements for the Student Ombudsman. For students who consider contacting the Student Ombudsman, it is often essential that they receive advice and guidance without disclosure. Therefore, the fact that a student representative is subject to the rules of the Administrative Act on professional secrecy is very important. Lawmaking student representatives will strengthen students' rights and help ensure a better student working day. I am therefore pleased that an overall committee is behind the recommendation to legislate student representatives.
ParlaMint-NO_2019-06-04.ana.ud396e3347	This is a good day. St. 16 for 2016–2017, better known as the quality notification. Through the Norwegian Research Council, Norway has joined Plan S, an international initiative for open access to all publicly funded research by 2020, recently deferred to 2021. The Committee notes that there appears to be widespread agreement in the sector on the objective of Plan S, but that there is some uncertainty about the practical implementation and consequences. On behalf of the Workers' Party, I would like to stress that open publication is an important objective. All publicly funded research should be open to all. That means democratization of knowledge. We believe that Norway has good conditions for effective open access work, but we see that much is unanswered about how Norwegian participation can best be implemented, and what consequences the connection will have. This – and the urgency of the Council of State – has created uncertainty and protest campaigns. What does Plan S mean, for example? copyright, recruitment of younger researchers and the position of the Norwegian professional language? The list is longer than that. In order to succeed with Plan S, it is essential to find good solutions and necessary transitional arrangements in close cooperation with the sector. Therefore, it is problematic that the government is in favour of rapid progress, limited information acquisition and late involvement of the parties and organisations. We in the Workers' Party cannot support such a process for implementing one of the most important reforms in the research world for a long time. ParlaMint-NO_2019-06-04.ana.ud396e3506	Plan S is important because open access to research results could contribute to stimulating the knowledge economy by providing several user groups with access to high-quality scientific literature. The availability of most of the research literature is limited today by its publication in subscription-based journals. National objectives and guidelines for open access to scientific articles provide that by 2024 all Norwegian scientific articles funded by public funds shall be open to the public. It shall be free for everyone to read and free of charge for individual researchers both to publish and to read research articles. At the same time, it is important that the implementation of Plan S does not restrict the ability of the professional community to publish research results in relevant channels. Therefore, the research sector needs to be involved in finding solutions that enable Norwegian researchers to meet the requirements resulting from Plan S without jeopardising the quality of research. In line with this, Coalition S, the International Consortium of Research Financiers, reports that it aims to carry out needs analyses and to contribute to the establishment and/or transition to journals in areas where there is a particular need for them. The fact that the Ministry of Knowledge’s political leadership is now in close dialogue with the sector, through both the University and Higher Education Councils and the research organisations, is crucial. This dialogue is also important in the implementation of Plan S, both in order to identify which journal is most important to open and in order to find appropriate funding models. So far, so-called publications and reading agreements have been secured with two of the largest market players through the ongoing publishing negotiations. That's good. Plan S shall not conflict with Norwegian legislation on copyright. State Council Nybø has asked Unit, in cooperation with the Norwegian Research Council and the Council of Universities and Higher Education Council, to investigate rights and licensing issues related to the open publication of scientific work and data sets in Norway, including issues relating to the responsibilities and rights of research institutions such as employers and issues related to the possible use of Creative Commons sharing licences, known as CC licences. The investigation shall be completed by 1 September 2019. It is reassuring that political leadership in the Ministry of Knowledge takes the researchers' concerns seriously with regard to Plan S, and we will follow the process closely. But we also need to be able to enjoy today that after decades of talking and good wishes for open publication, Plan S is finally being launched to achieve just this.

We want to hear the sector because the job they're going to do is influenced by how we reward the different publications in the future.

For interim declarations as referred to in the fourth paragraph of Section 165, this applies only if the declaration is to be submitted to the court. The obligation to transmit information also applies to statements on legal psychiatric matters drawn up by persons who are the parties to the proceedings under Section 149 if the statement is to be submitted to the court. § 165, second paragraph, No. 2 and 3 are replaced by the following: 2. compulsory care under Section 63 of the Criminal Code; or 3. maintenance of response as referred to in paragraph 1; 1 and 2 under Section 65 of the Criminal Code. § 165, second paragraph, No. the 4th is deleted; Paragraph 165, fourth paragraph, is replaced by the following: If there is doubt as to whether a judicial psychiatric examination is necessary, the prosecution authority or the court may decide to obtain a preliminary statement from an expert for guidance on this matter. The requirement for stability in the second paragraph of Section 165(a) applies as far as possible to the expert. Paragraph 165(a) is added as follows: Paragraph 165(a) Two experts shall be appointed to carry out a judicial psychiatric examination under the first and second paragraphs of Section 165, unless the court finds that there is sufficient one or that the case requires more than two. One of the experts must be an authorised specialist. If several experts have been appointed, one of them must work clinically. The person who would have been involved as a judge in the case under § 106 or 108 of the Law of the Court of Justice shall not be appointed as an expert for the purpose of carrying out a judicial psychiatric investigation under the first and second paragraphs of Section 165. Nomination should be avoided where the link with another expert in the case is appropriate to weaken the confidence of the expert. Before appointment, the expert shall submit a declaration of his own motion to the court. In the self-declaration, the expert shall disclose his/her education, clinical experience and matters relevant to the stability and independence assessment referred to in the second subparagraph. The King may prescribe requirements on the competence of experts, the self-declaration of experts, on requirements for the psychiatric declaration and on the tasks of the National Unit for the Psychiatric Expertise. Paragraph 167, first paragraph, second and new third and fourth sentences are replaced by the following: The Court of First Instance shall set a time limit for the duration of the application. The time limit shall be as short as possible and shall not exceed eight weeks. That period may be extended by a period of up to four weeks at a time. Paragraph 168(a) is added as follows: Necessary information relating to the health of the intended person may be divulged to the experts carrying out a judicial psychiatric examination, even if the intended person does not consent to such disclosure. This is only true in cases of offences that may result in prison for more than six years and where the outcome may depend on whether or not the intention is probable. IV In Law 20 May 2005 No 28 on punishment shall be amended as follows: Section 20 is replaced by the following: § 20 Debtability The person who, at the time of action, is under 15 years of age is not liable for criminal law. The same applies to those who, at the time of action, are unpredictable because of: a) strongly abnormal state of mind b) severe disturbance of consciousness; or c) a high degree of mental retardation. The uncertainty assessment referred to in the second subparagraph shall focus on the degree of failure of reality and functionality. Anyone who is transitory because of self-inflicted intoxication is not exempted from punishment, unless specific reasons warrant it. Any person who has a persistent condition referred to in point (a) of the second subparagraph and who causes an irregularity by himself may be punished for specific reasons. Paragraph 25, third paragraph, is replaced by the following: It is disregarded for ignorance as a result of self-inflicted intoxication. In such cases, the offender is judged to have been sober. Similarly, if he was in a self-inflicted unaccountability condition. Paragraph 62 is replaced by the following: § 62 Conditions to be imposed on the transfer to mandatory mental health protection A criminal who is unreliable under the second to fourth subparagraphs of Section 20 may, by judgment, be transferred to mandatory mental health protection when he has committed or attempted to commit a crime that violates the life, health or freedom of others or could endanger those assets, the special reaction is necessary to protect society, and the danger of a new and serious breach of integrity is imminent. Transfer to forced mental health protection may also be sentenced to a criminal offence which is unreliable under the second to fourth subparagraphs of Section 20 when it has committed repeated offences of a socially harmful or particularly burdensome nature, the special reaction is necessary to protect society from such offences, the risk of new offences of the same nature is particularly close, and other measures have clearly proved inappropriate. In assessing the recurrence risk, attention shall be paid to the offence committed in particular in relation to the other behaviour of the offender, the history of disease, the current mental state and the relationship with drugs. Transfer to forced mental health protection can also be condemned when the state of irregularity has resulted in the offender being in actual error, cf. Paragraph 25, or that the offender was otherwise in a condition that is incompatible with having continued. Paragraph 63, first paragraph, is replaced by the following: Under the conditions referred to in Section 62, the person who is unreliable under the second to fourth subparagraph of Section 20 may be subject to compulsory care. Paragraph 65, third paragraph, second sentence, is replaced by the following: In the event of a special reaction on the basis of the offences referred to in the second paragraph of Section 62, no termination may be sought within six months of the final judgment of the transfer or of the judgment refusing to terminate. Paragraph 65, fourth paragraph, third sentence, is replaced by the following: Special reaction on the basis of offences referred to in the second paragraph of Section 62 shall expire at the latest three years after the transfer judgment. Paragraph 78(d) is replaced by the following: d) at the time of action, the offender has reduced a sense of reality due to abnormal state of mind, mild mental retardation, disturbance of consciousness or a strong movement of mind; Paragraphs 80(f) and (g) are replaced by the following: f) at the time of action has a significant deterioration in the understanding of reality due to a highly abnormal state of mind, mental retardation or disturbance of consciousness, but is not unpredictable under the second paragraph of Section 20; (g) has acted under self-indebtedness or in a self-indebtedness condition under the fourth paragraph of Section 20 and there are particularly mitigating circumstances; The current letters i and j become letters h and i. ParlaMint-NO_2019-06-04.ana.ud396e7188	The surprise was great today early when we got the idea that the presidency with the three-to-three votes had decided to postpone the treatment of Prop. 81 S for 2018-2019 on the deployment of E18 in Telemark and Prop. 82 S for 2018-2019 on the deployment of E6 in Trøndelag. The two cases were submitted to the Transport Committee on 28 May – as planned. It has always been suggested that the two issues should be debated in Stortinget on 5 June. And until this morning it has been on the Stortinget meeting program. Earlier today, according to the NRK, Prime Minister Tone Wilhelmsen Trøen must have stated: ‘A majority of the Transport Committee has applied to the Presidency to move the debate date for two cases until 19 June.’;» This matter has never been dealt with in the Committee, i.e. to postpone the date of the debate. Later today we had committee meetings. It was not on the agenda, but the opposition parties raised the matter under any circumstances. It was explained that the government parties in the transport committee came up with this last night, and it was in principle that the government parties now believe that the two mentioned toll cases should be dealt with together with the third major toll case being dealt with in the Stortinget, namely the development of E6 in Hedmark and Oppland. Prop. 119 S for 2018-2019 on the development of E6 in Hedmark and Oppland was presented by the government on 24 May – four days before the committee decided on the other two cases, which were therefore to be dealt with tomorrow. Here's something that's not right. The government parties had had plenty of time to suggest that all three cases should be dealt with on the same day in Stortinget. It could have been suggested at an earlier time, but this was something that came to mind last night – and it is basically that you want the three cases to be dealt with at the same time, it is claimed. It is probably only the representatives of the government parties in the transport committee and the three members who represent the majority of the Presidency of the Grand Duchy who can at all try to argue that this has nothing to do with tomorrow's crisis meeting in the Progress Party. Yes, perhaps also the Prime Minister, because she has confirmed during the day today that she has been involved in the decision of the presidency. I find it a pity that the majority of the presidency can be used in such a party political game instead of standing up and dealing with the matter in principle. Is it now that if you find it unpleasant to deal with a matter in Stortinget, it is only to send an e-mail to the prime minister, and then the matter is taken by the map and dealt with in a later meeting? Here we have to get a reason from the presidency's majority for what's been going on in the back room. Why was it all of a sudden completely necessary to put the two toll cases at risk?
ParlaMint-NO_2019-06-04.ana.ud396e7208	The Presidency today considered at an early stage a request from the parties which are in the majority of the Committee on Transport and Communications to move the debate into two cases from 5 June to 19 June. The majority of the presidency made a decision that matters will be dealt with in the Stortinget on 19 June, at the same time as Prop. 119 S for 2018–2019. The majority of the Presidency supported the justification given by a majority of the parties to the Committee on Transport and Communications that it would be neat and appropriate to consider all the proposals on road projects financed by tolls on the same day of debate. The Presidency examines a number of requests and requests for a deferred deadline and changes to the dates of the debate. Similarly, today's request by a majority of the parties to the Committee was also examined in the Presidency. In accordance with the third paragraph of Section 7 of the Rules of Procedure, the vote of the Grand President is decisive in the form of a vote, and, as the representative Myrli referred to, the presidency's vote ended with three votes in favour, and then my vote, the vote of the Grand President, is decisive.

ParlaMint-NO_2019-06-04.ana.ud396e7220	As the representative of Myrli explained, the Committee on Transport and Communications has three toll proposals for consideration this summer. Two of them have been released, and it is correct, as the representative Myrli says, that they were initially set up for treatment tomorrow, Wednesday. The third is to be examined in the Committee and will be examined and released before the summer and is initially scheduled for consideration on 19 June. From our perspective, based on the discussions we have had between the government parties in the Transport Committee – Right, Advance Party and Left – we have come to the conclusion that we think it would be useful for the three toll proposals to be dealt with at the same time. We see that we will certainly have a rather significant toll debate linked to the toll proposals, which will go far beyond what is really going on, namely to finance these projects. We look forward to such a debate, but we think it is appropriate that we deal with this on the same day and may have an overall debate. That's why we proposed, and asked the President-in-Office, to move the two toll proposals that were set up for treatment tomorrow, Wednesday, to take them on the same day, on June 19. It has been honoured by the President-in-Office and the presidency, and I am pleased. I look forward to having a good and meaningful debate related to the three projects, but I am quite convinced that we will also have a quite comprehensive toll debate, which is natural as the political landscape looks right now.
ParlaMint-NO_2019-06-04.ana.ud396e7229	On 9 April, the government – with the Right and the Advance Party at its head – presented to the Stortinget proposals for two toll projects, two projects for which more than 10 billion will be required. kr in increased tolls. The matter was handed out by the Committee over a week ago, and let me make it clear: the proposal was presented by a Progress Party Council, approved by a Progress Party Finance Minister, and the vote clearly shows that the Advance Party Group fully supports the call for these 10 billion. kr in increased toll charges. I have to say it is very remarkable that the matter is so proposed deferred. Why will the presidency put forward a matter for the Government of the United Kingdom, for which the government has been able to take effect in the United Kingdom, a matter which has been dealt with in the Committee for a long time? It's not that one has a lot of that kind of enquiries, as the president said. Paragraph 35 of the Rules of Procedure requires much more to change the next week's programme in Stortinget. The question has to be: Will it from now on become common practice to postpone cases in which one of the government parties suddenly seems uncomfortable to vote for their own proposals and for their own policies just on one particular day? Or – more precisely – it is that one now adds that the Progress Party instead of voting for 10 billion. kr in increase tomorrow and 5 billion. kr in two weeks to vote for 15 br. kr in increased tolls in two weeks? That's what would have been the reality if this were a case. If this is a matter, if the government wants to change the policy, it would be natural to ask that the matter be drawn, or it would be natural to promote new proposals in the matter. The fact that the Prime Minister intervenes in the proceedings in Stortinget is quite amazing. It shouldn't be the presidency of the Storting, because the presidency is to be the presidency of the whole Biggest, not for half the Biggest. The presidency of the Great Britain should not be used in a purely political game. This case is embarrassing, just plain and simple.
